EmoBias providing the best fit to the data would suggest that incidental emotion cues influenced learning from integral reward outcomes at a decaying rate over time.

In the end, EmoBias offered a sample-level cognitive mechanism for the accuracy results reported above: the influence of emotion cues on reward learning decayed over time. Because EmoBias best explained the data overall (but see Supplemental Figure S6 for some best-fit variability across age), we extracted participants' Q-value and parameter estimates from their fitted EmoBias models and subjected them to further analyses. Interrogating Q-value estimates allowed us to characterize agerelated changes in the individual value representations that learning operated on. For each participant, we computed their trial-wise "Q-differences" within each decision pair by subtracting the Q-value estimate of the low-reward (25% reinforced) face deck from the high-reward (75% reinforced) face deck. A Qdifference of 0.50 reflected the reinforcement schedule of the task. Building upon Figure 2, Figure 3 shows that adolescents (13 to 17 years) and adults (18 to 22 years) best learned the reinforcement schedule of the task (Q-differences from all conditions concentrated near 0.50 in late trials), concurring with the age-related increases in accuracy reported above. Children (8 to 12 years), meanwhile, showed facilitation in reward learning by emotion-reward congruence (consistently high H+F-line) and learning interference by emotion-reward incongruence (consistently low F+H-line) throughout the task. Adolescents and adults replicated these patterns early in learning but reached almost completely (adolescents) or mostly (adults) precise value representations by the end of the task.

Inspecting EmoBias refined our accuracy conclusions: the congruency of information sources impacted children longer than adolescents or adults. When value representations were operationalized as binary accuracy (i.e., chose high-reward face deck), age and condition did not significantly interact to predict accuracy. Ultimately, children, adolescents, and adults made optimal decisions in all conditions. However, when value representations were treated continuously (i.e., Q-value differences of decks uncovered by the sample-level, best-fitting computational model that incorporated both emotion and reward information into value estimates), age and condition did interact. Figure 3 demonstrates that throughout learning, children represented the values of options as distinctly as adolescents and adults when emotion and reward information were congruent (H+F-condition; H+ is ~0.50 greater than F-in all age groups). In contrast, when emotion and reward information were incongruent (F+H-condition), adolescents and adults evinced more distinct value representations by the end of learning than children (F+ is ~0.25 greater than H-in adolescents and adults but only ~0.10 greater in children). When children made an optimal choice in the incongruent condition in the accuracy mixed-effects model, it was because their value representation for F+ was narrowly greater than H-; when adolescents and adults made an optimal choice, it was because their value representations mirrored the reinforcement schedule of the task.

Choice determinism increasing with age was also reflected in the fitted inverse temperature parameters. We extracted these 𝛽s from EmoBias for each participant, and a beta-distributed regression predicting 𝛽 from age found that 𝛽 indeed grew from childhood to adulthood (𝛽 = 0.083, p < 0.001; Supplemental Figure S5).

Reward Associations Persisted to Bias Choice with Increasing Age

To investigate how emotional expressions and reward associations persisted to bias choice across age, we fit a series of linear mixed-effects models to test phase decisions (i.e., when every option from the learning task was paired with every other and participants were asked to select which they thought was best, but they no longer received feedback for their choices). First, the mean frequency of choosing a particular face deck during the test phase was predicted by age, its reward association, its emotional expression, and their interactions. A random intercept for participant ID accounted for repeated measurements. Through analyses of deviance, we found main effects of reward (c 2 (1) = 271.480, p < 0.001) and emotion (c 2 (1) = 74.220, p < 0.001); 75% reinforced face decks were chosen more frequently than 25% reinforced face decks and happy face decks were chosen more frequently than fear face decks. A two-way interaction between age and reward specified that high-reward face decks were chosen more frequently than low-reward face decks with increasing age (c 2 (1) = 62.543, p < 0.001; Figure 4A). Converging evidence was produced by an analogous linear mixed-effects model predicting the mean response time (RT) for choosing a particular face in the test phase from age, reward, emotion, and their interactions; a random intercept for participant ID accounted for repeated measurements. Again, we found main effects of reward (c 2 (1) = 66.673, p < 0.001) and emotion (c 2 (1) = 10.508, p < 0.01); 25% reinforced faces were chosen more slowly than 75% reinforced faces and fear faces were chosen more slowly than happy faces. A two-way interaction between age and reward specified that low-reward faces were chosen more slowly than high-reward faces with increasing age (c 2 (1) = 38.899, p < 0.001; Figure 4B). Both the frequency and RT model formulations revealed that the "carry-over" of prior, goal-relevant reward associations to current, non-reinforced decision contexts became more prevalent into adulthood. Further converging evidence was produced by valence ratings of the emotional face stimuli (Supplemental Results).

As an index of deliberation, RT can also be used to infer choice option similarity in decisionmaking tasks. Choices with similar options are thought to require more deliberation and thus longer RTs, while choices with distinct options are thought to require less deliberation and thus shorter RTs. Leveraging this framework, we next fit a linear mixed-effects model with age, match type (i.e., whether the face decks were reward-matched, emotion-matched, fully-matched, or fully-mismatched), and their interactions predicting RT during novel test phase trials (i.e., not pairings from the learning task). A random intercept for participant ID accounted for repeated measurements. Through analyses of deviance, we found a main effect of match type (c 2 (3) = 54.627, p < 0.001); the longest RTs were observed for fullymatched pairs (i.e., decks with the same reward associations and emotional expressions), and these RTs were driven by reward-more than emotion-matching. This main effect was qualified by a two-way interaction between age and match type (c 2 (3) = 47.177, p < 0.001; Figure 4C), and targeted post-hoc tests of estimated marginal means (p-values Holm-corrected for multiple comparisons; Supplemental Table S9) probed significant differences between match patterns at instructive age values (child = 10.00 years, adolescent = 15.50 years, adult = 21.00 years). Longer RTs were observed for fully-matched and emotion-matched pairs in children but fully-matched and reward-matched pairs in adults: during adolescence, deliberation changed from emotion-dominated to reward-dominated. Additional measures verified that these consistent signatures of reward-based choice emerging with age were not because younger participants lacked implicit knowledge of the reward associations (Supplemental Results). Rather, an improvement of explicit knowledge of the reward associations into adulthood may explain these age-related changes in leveraging previously goal-relevant information for reward-based choice (Supplemental Results). S9) between fullymatched pairs and other match types in estimated marginal means tests at targeted ages. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines.

Discussion

Together, our study leveraged several tasks, self-report measures, and modeling approaches to examine how incidental emotion cues and integral reward outcomes shape choice from childhood to adulthood. Across these measures and their analyses, emotion-reward incongruence interfered with early learning in participants of all ages, suggesting integration of emotion and reward information into a common value representation. However, adolescents' and adults' eventual prioritization of the goalrelevant reward associations in the learning task and in other indices of decision strategies highlights that the predominating influence of integral over incidental information on choice emerges with age.

Aligning with previous research indicating that adults calibrate their learning to the reinforcement statistics of their environments more optimally than younger individuals (38), we found that learning task accuracy improved across time, especially for older participants (46). While participants of all ages were affected by emotion-reward incongruence, incidental emotion cues impacted children's Q-value estimates longer than older participants' Q-values estimates. This result is consistent with prior work demonstrating that irrelevant information sources impede early learning in all ages (27, 47), but behavioral adjustments that accord with prospective monetary rewards increase into adulthood (24, 34, 35). Our study amplifies the idea that making goal-directed, reward-maximizing decisions, and learning from them, is tuned in adolescence (1, 28).

The Q-value estimates offered another key insight: adolescents tended to disregard interfering information sources and instead relied on personally experienced, integral reward outcomes more than other ages. This adolescent advantage has been reported in some reward learning work (39, 40), including studies in which participants needed to overcome distracting information sources to make goaldirected choices (27). For example, adolescents show a reduced Pavlovian bias (act to earn rewards, withhold action to avoid losses) on instrumental learning (48, but see refs. 49 and 50). However, the fact that we show adolescents recovering almost completely from emotional interference in reward learning is striking, given that emotion information is thought to be particularly salient for this age group (25). Even though emotional expressions have been found to disrupt higher cognition most during the teenage years (13, 14), here we observe the strongest prioritization of goal-relevant reward information during adolescence and adulthood.

Ultimately, the learning task and its statistical and computational models revealed that while incidental emotional expressions interfered initially with making optimal choices across age, adolescents and adults showed the greatest propensity to filter out those distracting cues to prioritize learning from integral reward outcomes. Age-emerging preferences for reward information were also observed in the test phase and supplemental self-report measures. In these non-reinforced decision contexts, children's choice preferences were guided by emotion cues, adults' choice preferences were guided by reward outcomes, and adolescents demonstrated a transition from emotion-to reward-guided choice preferences. There are two possible explanations for this developmental transition. On the one hand, and as discussed above, this could be explained by increases in goal-directed behavior during adolescence and into adulthood (1, 28, 51): reward outcomes were integral to participants' previous learning goals while emotion cues were incidental. Alternatively, this could be explained by the conspicuousness of the emotional expressions as an information source. Reward associations had to be acquired during the learning task and retained, while the emotional expressions were simply visible on the screen -they were immediately available to use. Cognitive control research has shown that children's behavior is most affected by an immediately available source of information, while adults' behavior is affected by both an immediately available source of information and a source whose meaning must be learned over time (24). Since reward information in this study is both integral and learnable and emotion information is both incidental and available, future work should follow the leads established here to disentangle goal-relevant vs. learnable dimensions of information and further examine the developmental transition from emotion to reward choice preferences.

Finally, while reinforcement learning improved into adulthood, participants of all ages did indeed learn. Supplemental analyses showed signatures of implicit knowledge of the reward associations from the learning task across the sample. Nonetheless, concurring with findings of metacognition increasing into adulthood (52), explicit knowledge of the reward structure improved with age. Goal achievement is a progression -from recognizing a goal as worthwhile, to formulating a plan to pursue it, to carrying out that plan. Previous research has shown that representing and prioritizing goals improves with age (51, 53, 54). Perhaps the shift to reward-based choice in adults was driven by their explicit recognition of goalrelevant information.

In addition to our primary focus on age-related change in the prioritization of goal-relevant information, this study addressed a longstanding issue of interest across age groups -how different affective signals combine into common representations of value. EmoBias being the best-fitting computational model indicates that learning was based on value representations comprising both emotion and reward information. Though these representations initially "defaulted" to integrating across affective signals, with early Q-value estimates reflecting reward outcomes biased by emotion expectations, this integration was not fixed. Ultimately, value was dynamically constructed, changing over time as integral rewards, relevant to the prescribed reinforcement learning goals, superseded incidental, but potent, emotion signals. Thus, the learning results support theories of value composition across information pg 10 streams, beginning even in late childhood, while also revealing an interesting moderator of affective salience in this integration process: goal-relevancy. This study's findings should be contextualized according to its sample composition and testing procedure. Our participants were typically developing children, adolescents, and adults from the United States who had access to a laptop or desktop computer with a reliable internet connection. Therefore, our results may not generalize to populations with relatively reduced technological access, or those with neurodivergent identities. Moreover, our study design was cross-sectional, sampling participants from 8 to 22 years, rather than measuring individual participants at several time points as they aged. Consequently, the age-related changes in emotion-and reward-influenced choice that we report here at the group level may not track at the individual level.

Notwithstanding, this work details age-related changes in the use of incidental emotion and integral reward information for goal-directed behavior. The fact that incidental emotion cues disrupted learning from integral reward outcomes in participants of all ages supports the integration of emotion and reward information into a common, affective value representation and highlights the potency of emotion as an information source. However, the fact that reward associations dominated later decision-making in adults more than children and adolescents emphasizes that goal-relevant information is privileged with increasing age. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that considering how affective information sources relate to goals (incidental vs. integral), in addition to what they are (emotion vs. reward), is critical for characterizing value-based choice across age. Resolving conflicting theories about the prioritization of incidental emotion vs. integral reward information through adolescence, goal relevancy explains the increasing use of reward information in learning and decision-making tasks into adulthood.

Materials and Methods

Participants

One hundred twenty-one participants ages 8 to 22 years completed this online study. They were evenly distributed across age and self-or parent/guardian-reported gender (Mage = 15.59 years, SDage = 4.44 years; 64 women, 56 men, 1 gender not captured by the available options; Supplemental Figure S1). Participants also self-or parent/guardian-reported their race (0.83% American Indian or Alaska Native, 18.18% Asian, 6.61% Black or African American, 57.02% White, 17.36% race not captured by available options) and ethnicity (15.70% Hispanic, 84.30% non-Hispanic). Six additional participants (10.16 to 20.62 years) were tested but not included in analyses because four failed task instruction comprehension checks (described below), one did not comply with testing procedures, and there was one testing error. The a priori target sample size (N = 120) exceeded that of prior studies detecting significant age-related behavioral change in probabilistic reinforcement learning tasks (e.g., refs. 27, 39, 47, 48, and 55). A posthoc, sensitivity power analysis confirmed that the primary learning task results could be detected at proportionally smaller sample sizes: we were >75% powered to detect all reported main effects and interactions for sample size decreases of up to 20% (Nsubsample = 96), and most results were robust to sample size decreases of 30% (Nsubsample = 84).

Participants were contacted from a database of families and through other means of community recruitment, then screened for exclusion criteria. They must have been fluent in English and living in the United States at the time of the study. Participants were also excluded if they used psychotropic medications; had current learning disabilities or other cognitive impairments; had autism, ADHD, or movement disorders; had current psychiatric or neurological illness diagnoses (except adjustment disorder); and if they did not have normal or corrected-to-normal vision and audition. Parents/guardians of minor participants must have been willing and able to provide informed permission. Minor and adult participants must have been willing and able to provide assent or consent, respectively. Permission, assent, and consent were obtained via online forms requiring digital signatures, and participants were encouraged to contact the research team with any questions.

Eligible participants could complete the study asynchronously at any time over the course of a week -independently and in one sitting. We logged study completion time and found that most participants completed the study in the afternoon or evening, and there was no significant relationship between age and completion time (submission hour ~ age: R² = 0.004, F(1, 118) = 0.422, p = 0.517). Participants were required to use a laptop or desktop computer with a Google Chrome web browser and affirmed their testing environment was free of distractions using a structured checklist (Supplemental Figure S2). All instructions were recorded and read aloud; a brief pre-study audio test ensured that these instructions played successfully (31). The study was hosted on Qualtrics and cognition.run and all tasks were programmed in jsPsych (version 8.0; 56). To evaluate whether there was unbalanced cognitive ability across participants as a product of age-related sampling biases or random chance, we administered the matrix reasoning subtest of the Wechsler Abbreviated Scale of Intelligence (57) and did not find evidence of this potential reward learning confound (age-adjusted t-score ~ age: R² = 0.003, F(1, 118) = 0.326, p = 0.569).

Participants were paid $20 for 90 minutes of their time and could receive up to $20 in performance-based bonus payments. Compensation was administered through a gift card of participants' choosing. The study protocol was approved by the Committee on the Use of Human Subjects at Harvard University.

Learning Task Measured the Interacting Influences of Incidental Emotion and Integral Reward Information on Choice

The probabilistic reinforcement learning task characterized how incidental emotion and integral reward information impacted choice. Moreover, it characterized whether these information sources operated independently or as a composite. The emotion cues and reward outcomes were implemented in a fully crossed within-subjects design to manipulate the congruence of the approach vs. avoidance tendencies that these information sources elicit. Participants were presented with eight unique card decks, grouped into four pairs ("conditions") that were pseudorandomly interspersed through the 180 trial task. During each trial (Figure 1A), participants saw a pair of decks, with one deck on the left side of the screen and the other deck on the right. Whether a deck appeared on the left or right side of the screen was random for each trial. Participants made choices between the card decks by pressing F for the left option and J for the right option. The choice was available on the screen for up to 30 seconds, and a warning ("CHOOSE NOW") was added to the screen if a participant did not respond within three seconds. Following their choices, participants' responses were highlighted and visible on the screen (up to three seconds if recorded before the time warning; three full seconds if recorded after the time warning). If a participant failed to respond within 30 seconds, they saw a "TOO SLOW" screen. We a priori planned to remove any participant from the study who received a "TOO SLOW" screen on at least 10% of trials, under the presumption of inattention. Ultimately, this criterion did not apply to anyone we tested.

The decks were distinguishable by the image of an actor displaying a facial expression commonly judged to depict one of two emotions, intended to invoke opposing approach vs. avoidance tendencies: happy (approach) vs. fear (avoid; 8, 9). No face was portrayed on more than one deck. The faces were selected from the Karolinska Directed Emotional Faces database (KDEF; 42), which consists of male and female Caucasian faces. The faces participants viewed were selected to match participants' self-or parent/guardian-reported gender but not their age or race due to natural limitations of the stimulus set. The KDEF faces used in the task were chosen according to the following validation criteria (58): target emotion identification rate, intensity rating, and most common labeling error. The faces had the highest emotion labeling accuracy by participants in ref. 58, and deck pairs were created between faces that were rated as similar in intensity as possible in ref. 58 (Supplemental Table S1). Fear faces that were most mistaken for anger were not used (as anger is an approach-inducing emotion; 59), nor were fear faces that were most mistaken for happy.

After a participant chose a deck during a trial, it "flipped over" for two seconds, revealing a 50 cent vs. 0 cent outcome, as well as a trial-unique image. The trial-unique images (see Supplemental Methods for selection criteria) were included for a surprise memory test administered at the end of the study (data not reported here). The monetary rewards were delivered through a probabilistic reinforcement schedule, such that there was an optimal choice in each pair of decks. Optimal choices were recorded as accurate; suboptimal choices were recorded as inaccurate. The optimal choice was reinforced with 50 cent outcomes 75% of the time and 0 cent outcomes 25% of the time (expected value of 37.50 cents), while the suboptimal choice was reinforced with 0 cent outcomes 75% of the time and 50 cent outcomes 25% of the time (expected value of 12.50 cents). Again, the reward structure intended to invoke opposing approach vs. avoidance tendencies: 75% reinforced (approach) vs. 25% reinforced (avoid). Participants were instructed to determine which decks were associated with greater monetary reward so that they could collect as many cents as possible; their performance-based bonus payment was 20% of their real earnings.

In each pair, the optimal and suboptimal decks were assigned faces with happy or fear emotional expressions in a 2x2 design. This created an orthogonal, within-subjects manipulation of emotion and reward across four conditions (Figure 1B). First, a high-reward happy face deck was paired with a lowreward fear face deck, invoking congruent approach-avoid tendencies (H+F-). Second, a high-reward fear face deck was paired with a low-reward happy face deck, invoking incongruent approach-avoid tendencies (F+H-). Third and fourth, high-and low-reward happy face decks were paired (H+H-) and high-and low-reward fear face decks were paired (F+F-). These emotion-matched conditions isolated participants' reward-based choice patterns. Each condition was presented 45 times across three blocks of 60 trials. Participants were allowed to take a short break between blocks.

Prior to the learning task, participants were required to pass three comprehension checks to ensure their logistical and conceptual understanding of the instructions. Four participants were excluded from analyses for failing these checks.

Test Phase Measured the Persistence of Emotion and Reward Information on Choice

The test phase was modified from refs. 27 and 47 to characterize the persistence of emotion and reward biases on choice. Immediately after the learning task, participants were presented with all 28 possible pairings of card decks (e.g., H+ from the H+F-learning task condition paired with H+ from H+H-), four times each, for a total of 112 test phase trials. During each trial, participants were instructed to choose which of the decks in the pair was better based on what they had learned earlier. The card decks in the pairs were randomly presented on the left and right sides of the screen. When cards were chosen during the test phase, they did not "flip over" to reveal a reward outcome; unlike in the learning task, no feedback was provided to participants in the test phase. Indeed, while there was a "correct" reward-based answer for some trials (e.g., H+ from H+F-paired with H-from H+H-), there was no "correct" rewardbased answer for other trials (e.g., H+ from H+F-paired with F+ from F+F-). The choice was available on the screen for up to 30 seconds, and a warning ("CHOOSE NOW") was added to the screen if a participant did not respond within ten seconds. If a participant failed to respond within 30 seconds, they saw a "TOO SLOW" screen. Several indices were extracted from the test phase: the frequency with which a particular face deck was chosen as preferred, the RT for choosing a particular face deck, and the RT for different types of test phase pairs (i.e., familiar test phase trials vs. novel test phase trials, including pairs of reward-matched, emotion-matched, fully-matched, and fully-mismatched face decks).

Mixed-Effects Models

Mixed-effects models were implemented in R (version 4.5.1; 60).

Model Fitting. Mixed-effects models from the lmerTest package (a wrapper for the lme4 package, plus additional inferential output; version 3.1.3; 61, 62) were applied to the learning task and test phase data. The learning task model with accuracy as its dependent variable was fit as a logistic mixed-effects analysis with the glmer function. The test phase models with frequency or RT as their respective dependent variables were fit as linear mixed-effects analyses with the lmer function. Wherever possible, the distributional assumptions of the models were appraised with qq-plots and posterior predictive checks (using the check_predictions function from the performance package; version 0.15.1; 63) and deemed fulfilled.

Random Effects Structure. In all mixed-effects models, participant ID was included as a random intercept to account for repeated measurements. The learning task accuracy model failed to converge with a more complex random effects structure -when condition was associated with a random slope. To keep the random effects structures consistent across models, random slopes were not included in the other analyses.

Post-Hoc Tests. Targeted post-hoc tests of estimated marginal means were performed on select mixedeffects models described above. These tests clarified, for key results, time and age points demonstrating significant differences. Tests were run using the emmeans, pairs, and contrast functions from the emmeans package (version 1.11.2.8; 64). For the learning task accuracy model, estimated marginal mean differences between all conditions were compared for instructive time points during the task: after the beginning (i.e., entering the first break; trial = 15), after the middle (i.e., entering the second break; trial = 30), and after the end (i.e., at the task's conclusion; trial = 45) of learning. For the test phase RT model fit to novel test phase trials, estimated marginal mean differences between fully-matched pairs and other match types were examined for instructive age values: near the youngest end (10.00 years), near pg 13 the oldest end (21.00 years), and at the middle of (15.50 years) our age range. P-values were corrected for multiple comparisons according to the Holm method.

Assessing Significance of Effects. Analyses of deviance (type II Wald χ2 tests with Satterthwaite approximations for degrees of freedom) using the Anova function from the car package (version 3.1.3; 65) were performed on the mixed-effects models described above. All main effects and interactions produced by the models demonstrating p < 0.05 were interpreted as statistically significant. These main effects and interactions were examined visually using the plot_model function from the sjPlot package (version 2.9.0; 66), as well as the ggpredict function from the ggeffects package (version 2.3.1; 67) in concert with ggplot functionality from the ggplot2 package (version 4.0.0; 68). Raw data visualizations were also implemented using ggplot functionality from the ggplot2 package (version 4.0.0; 68).

Temporal Difference Computational Models

Model Fitting. Temporal difference computational models (44, 45) were adapted from reinforcement learning studies with similarly-aged participants (48, 69) and implemented in MATLAB (version R2022b; 70). Models were fit to participants' learning task data by maximizing the log posterior of their choices with the fmincon function. Model comparison accounted for several metrics: lowest mean AIC, lowest median AIC, highest number of participants for whom the model explained the most variance, and greatest variance explained above and beyond a random choice policy. Because our participants ranged from children to adults, it was important to balance these model comparison metrics. While one model may have been best-fitting at the sample level (e.g., indicated by lowest mean AIC), a different model may have been best-fitting at the individual level (e.g., indicated by highest number of participants for whom the model explained the most variance). If we prioritized one metric over another, we would risk choosing a winning model that fit different age groups to different degrees.

Parameter Estimation. The number of free parameters varied by model. Alpha parameters in all models were constrained between 0 and 1. Omega parameters, in applicable models, were also constrained between 0 and 1. Beta parameters in all models were constrained between 0 and 30.

Age Differences in EmoBias Parameter Estimates. Generalized linear models were implemented in R (version 4.5.1; 60) with the glmmTMB function from the glmmTMB package (version 1.1.13; 71, 72) to examine age differences in alpha, beta, and omega parameters extracted from the best-fitting EmoBias model. Models predicted alpha, beta, or omega from age. The parameter estimates were normalized between 0 and 1 and the family arguments accommodated the beta-distributed estimates. The distributional assumptions of the models were appraised with qq-plots (residuals simulated with the DHARMa package; version 0.4.7; 73) and posterior predictive checks (using the check_predictions function from the performance package; version 0.15.1; 63) and deemed fulfilled. The significance of effects was assessed with the summary function from the lmerTest package (version 3.1.3; 61, 62). Effects demonstrating p < 0.05 were interpreted as statistically significant. Main effects and raw data were visualized using the tools described above.

Trial-Unique Images

Immediately above the choice feedback (50 vs. 0 cents) in the learning task, trial-unique images were included on the back of each card deck after it "flipped over". Every trial-unique image was selected from the object categories database examined in ref. 2. Only object categories that were not associated with facial emotions and did not carry an emotional (e.g., a threatening axe) or motivational (e.g., a monetarily rewarding coin) charge were chosen. Additionally, object categories were only considered if they were deemed, according to two members of the research team, comprehensible by children 8 years and older. Finally, any obscure or outdated object categories, or categories that were exceedingly similar to others, were omitted. From each of the acceptable object categories, objects centered in the image with the most prototypical appearance, least amount of text, least pattern, most high-contrast colors, least negative space, and least pixelation were selected to display on the card backs.

Temporal Difference Computational Models

We implemented nine total temporal difference computational models. The baseline (Vanilla) and best-fitting (EmoBias) models are described in the main text; the remainder are described below.

EmoQ Model. Q-values were initialized to 1 for happy and 0 for fear faces. From there, learning proceeded according to the Vanilla model specification. EmoQ providing the best fit to the data would suggest that incidental emotion cues influenced the "start point" of integral reward learning. EmoHier Model. Two Q-value estimates were maintained. Reward Q-values were initialized to 0.50, but emotion Q-values were initialized to 1 for happy and 0 for fear faces. Before a decision, a random number between 0 and 1 was generated. This random number 𝐾 represented the probability of engaging an emotion (𝐸) vs. reward (𝑅) learning system. If 𝐾 was below 𝜙, then participants updated the emotion Q-value 𝑄(𝑑 % ); if 𝐾 was above 𝜙, then participants updated the reward Q-value 𝑄(𝑑 0 ): was below 𝜙, then emotion Q-values were converted to choice probabilities via a softmax function; if 𝐾 was above 𝜙, then reward Q-values were converted to choice probabilities via a softmax function: 7) 7∈:

E

EmoHier providing the best fit to the data would suggest that participants arbitrated between separate emotion and reward learning systems.

EmoAvg Model. Two Q-value estimates were maintained. Reward Q-values were initialized to 0.50, but emotion Q-values were initialized to 1 for happy and 0 for fear faces. Before a decision, the average of the reward and emotion Q-values was taken. Average Q-values were converted to choice probabilities via a softmax function. random sampling; this was observed in all models. Low correlations between output parameters ("out" variables) indicates a lack of "tradeoff" between the parameters during the fitting process; this also was observed in all models. Where applicable, "1" refers to a parameter fit to the first condition (H+F-), "2" refers to a parameter fit to the second condition (H+H-), "3" refers to a parameter fit to the third condition (F+F-), and "4" refers to a parameter fit to the fourth condition (F+H-).

Mixed-Effects Models

Mixed-effects models were implemented in R (version 4.5.1; 4). Model Fitting. Mixed-effects models from the lmerTest package (a wrapper for the lme4 package, plus additional inferential output; version 3.1.3; 5, 6) or glmmTMB package (version 1.1.13; 7, 8) were applied to the test phase and self-report measure data. The test phase, subjective valence rating, and reinforcement report models with RT, rating difference, or reinforcement estimate as their respective dependent variables were fit as linear mixed-effects analyses with the lmer function. The subjective value of money model with ratings as its dependent variable was fit as a generalized mixed-effects analysis with the glmmTMB function; the family argument accommodated a beta distribution. Wherever possible, the distributional assumptions of the models were appraised with qq-plots and posterior predictive checks (using the check_predictions function from the performance package; version 0.15.1; 9) and deemed fulfilled. We assessed the significance of effects using the tools described in the main text. We also visualized main effects, interactions, and raw data using the tools described in the main text.

Through analyses of deviance, we found main effects of reward (c 2 (1) = 166.276, p < 0.001) and emotion (c 2 (1) = 17.047, p < 0.001); 75% reinforced faces were reported as yielding 50 cent outcomes more frequently than 25% reinforced faces and happy faces were reported as yielding 50 cent outcomes more frequently than fear faces. A two-way interaction between age and reward specified that high-reward faces were reported as yielding 50 cent outcomes more frequently than low-reward faces with increasing age (c 2 (1) = 19.473, p < 0.001; Supplemental Figure S10). S10. Greater explicit knowledge of relative reward associations with age. High-reward faces were reported as yielding 50 cent outcomes more frequently than low-reward faces into adolescence and adulthood, even though individuals of all ages experienced the same 75% and 25% reinforcement rates. As seen by the range of the y-axis, participants of all ages under-reported the number of reward outcomes they received (on average, high-reward faces yielded 33.75 50 cent outcomes and low-reward faces yielded 11.25 50 cent outcomes). Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines.

Supplemental Figure

While the main effect of reward provides evidence for some degree of explicit knowledge of the reward structure across age, the interaction between age and reward indicates that explicit knowledge of relative reward rates does improve into adulthood. Do these results reflect developmental changes in the awareness of reward outcomes, or can they be explained by agerelated changes in the subjective valuation of money? In other words, are adults more aware of when they have vs. have not received 50 cent outcomes simply because they value 50 cent outcomes more than children? To address this potential confound, participants rated how much small, learning task-relevant amounts of money ($0.00, $0.25, $0.50, $0.75, $1.00) were (from "not much money" to "a lot of money") in a subjective value of money scale administered before the task. These data were fit with a generalized linear (beta-distributed) mixed-effects model in which ratings were predicted by age, amount, and their interactions. A random intercept for participant ID accounted for repeated measurements. Through analyses of deviance, we found main effects of age (c 2 (1) = 8.468, p < 0.01) and amount (c 2 (4) = 929.532, p < 0.001); ratings decreased with age and were greater for higher amounts of money. A two-way interaction between age and amount specified that ratings differed by each amount of money in younger more than older participants (c 2 (4) = 119.936, p < 0.001; Supplemental Figure S11). Though there may be developmental changes in the evaluation of small amounts of money, if anything, children demonstrate enhanced differentiation of reward outcomes in a task-agnostic subjective value of money scale but reduced differentiation of reward outcomes in their task-dependent reinforcement ratings.

Parameter Recovery

Parameter and model recovery were performed to assess whether the candidate bestfitting models produced stable parameter estimates and captured distinct cognitive mechanisms (3). For each model (Vanilla, EmoBias, Cond, CondBeta), we simulated N = 121 participants by randomly sampling (with replacement) free parameters from the empirical distribution of actual participants' estimates and trial sequences experienced by actual participants. After using those parameters and sequences to generate data under each model, we fit the models to their own data, as well as the data generated from every other model. S4. All models demonstrated parameter recovery: randomly sampled input parameters ("in" variables) correlated with fitted output parameters ("out" variables) at rs > 0.6 (red diagonal in every panel). Low correlations between input parameters ("in" variables) indicates effective Model Recovery Supplemental Table S4. Mean AIC values. Self-Report Measures Captured Subjective Experiences of Emotional Expressions and Monetary Rewards

Subjective Valence Ratings. Before the learning task and after the test phase, participants were instructed to evaluate the faces on the card decks. These valence evaluations (verbatim: "Rate how this face makes you feel") were provided along 7-point Likert scales (from "Very negative" to "Very positive"). A valence rating difference was computed for each face to capture how feelings changed after monetary rewards were associated with the emotional expressions during learning.

Reinforcement Reports. After the test phase, participants were instructed to report how many times they thought they received 50 cents for choosing each card deck during the learning task. These reinforcement estimates were provided along continuous scales from 0 to 45 and captured explicit knowledge of reward outcomes for faces bearing emotional expressions.

Subjective Value of Money Scale. Before the learning task, participants were instructed to endorse how valuable different amounts of money ($0.00, $0.25, $0.50, $0.75, $1.00) were to them. Importantly, these amounts included those used in the task. These ratings were provided along continuous scales from 0 to 100 (anchored by "not much money" to "a lot of money") and captured general interpretations of small monetary rewards.

Ancillary Learning Strategies Changed with Age

Although we found evidence for emotion-biased reward learning strategies at the sample level (EmoBias as the winning model subject to further analyses), there was some best-fit variability across age groups. Examining the number of participants who produced the lowest AIC values for each of the models (Supplemental Figure S6), we see that children (8-12 years) also often engaged in reward-based learning while adolescents (13-17 years) and adults (18-22 years) also often engaged in condition-wise learning. This pattern of model fits indicates that incidental emotion information shaped goal-directed behavior similarly across age, but access to contextual learning strategies emerged during adolescence. S6. Best-fitting model by model type and age group. Candidate best-fitting model types were EmoBias (incidental emotion cues influenced learning from integral reward outcomes at a decaying rate over time), Cond (participants utilized different degrees of value updating in each condition), and CondBeta (participants utilized different degrees of choice determinism in each condition); baseline model type was Vanilla (learning only from integral reward outcomes). Participant counts of lowest AIC values favoring EmoBias were high in every age group, but participant counts favoring Vanilla were also high in children while participant counts favoring Cond were also high in adolescents and adults. Participants were binned into children (8-12 years), adolescents (13-17 years), and adults (18-22 years) to interpret changing patterns of best-fitting models across age.

Supplemental Figure

Children often making unidimensional decisions (i.e., engaging in reward-based learning) aligns with previous research concluding that, when faced with dual information streams, children's choices are shaped by a single stream, while adults' choices reflect both streams (10). Indeed, choice strategies are thought to become more sophisticated with age (11). Adolescents and adults were frequently well-fit by models formalizing condition-wise decisions, which incorporated both emotion and reward information. Older participants often engaging in contextbased learning aligns with earlier studies finding age-related increases in the use of reward learning strategies that reflect the structure, or a model, of the environment, rather than proximal outcomes (12) (13) (14).

Reward Associations Persisted to Bias Face Valence Ratings with Increasing Age

The test phase, reported in the main text, indexed how reward outcomes persisted to bias choices about emotion cues across age. To complement this measure, comparisons of the pre-to post-task subjective valence ratings (from1 = "Very negative" to7 = "Very positive") captured how reward outcomes persisted to bias appraisals of emotion cues. In this linear mixedeffects model, the valence rating difference (post minus pre) for each face was predicted by age, its reward association, its emotional expression, and their interactions. A random intercept for participant ID accounted for repeated measurements. Through analyses of deviance, we found significant main effects of reward (c 2 (1) = 25.702, p < 0.001) and emotion (c 2 (1) = 11.807, p < 0.001); 75% reinforced faces became rated more positively pre-to post-learning while 25% reinforced faces became rated more negatively, and fear faces became rated relatively more positively than happy faces. Learned reward associations changing emotional appraisals provides evidence for emotion and reward information converging into a common value representation from childhood to adulthood. A two-way interaction between age and reward further specified that high-reward faces were rated more positively and low-reward faces were rated more negatively with increasing age (c 2 (1) = 16.227, p < 0.001; Supplemental Figure S7). As in the test phase, the subjective valence ratings revealed that the "carry-over" of prior, goal-relevant reward associations to current appraisals gained prevalence into adulthood. Another two-way interaction between age and emotion specified that fear faces became rated relatively more positively than happy faces with increasing age (c 2 (1) = 5.027, p < 0.05; Supplemental Figure S8); repeated experience viewing the faces and/or their reward associations could have induced shifts in emotion appraisals in older but not younger participants, especially for fear faces. S7. Reward associations changed valence ratings of faces more with age. High-reward faces were rated more positively and low-reward faces were rated more negatively into adulthood, even though individuals of all ages experienced the same 75% and 25% reinforcement rates. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines.

Supplemental Figure S8. Face valence changes by age and emotional expression.

With increasing age, repeated experience viewing the faces and/or their reward associations induced more positive appraisals (i.e., valence changes > 0) of faces, but especially for fear faces. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines. S9. Targeted post-hoc tests of estimated marginal means (p-values Holmcorrected for multiple comparisons) revealed age-varying differences between match patterns. Cells with statistically significant tests (p < 0.05) are highlighted.

Reward-Based Choice Emerged with Age, Perhaps Due to Improvements in Explicit Knowledge of Reward Associations into Adulthood

All test phase model formulations reported in the main text demonstrated that rewardbased choice emerged with age: into adulthood, high-reward face decks were chosen more frequently, low-reward faces were chosen more slowly, and RTs for fully-matched and rewardmatched pairs converged. Was this because of an increasing preference for goal-relevant reward information into adulthood, or because younger participants lacked implicit or explicit knowledge of the reinforcement structure of the learning task? To investigate whether participants demonstrated implicit knowledge of reward associations after the learning task, we again turned to RT as an index of deliberation in the test phase (i.e., when every option from the learning task was paired with every other and participants were asked to select which they thought was best, but they no longer received feedback for their choices). We fit a linear mixed-effects model with age, condition, and their interactions predicting RT during familiar test phase trials (i.e., pairings that were present in the learning task). A random intercept for participant ID accounted for repeated measurements. Analyses of deviance revealed no significant main effects or interactions (ps > 0.580; Supplemental Figure S9). Test phase RT did not vary by age, or what condition the choice was in the learning task, indicating that these familiar trials required comparable deliberation across age. Comparable deliberation is interpreted to mean that from childhood to adulthood, the options were similarly distinct in every condition. Consistent with the learning task's accuracy mixed-effects model (i.e., > 50% response accuracy by the end of the task for every age group, for every condition), this test phase model corroborated that reward learning did eventually occur in all ages: implicit knowledge of reward associations was apparent throughout the sample by the end of the learning task. S9. Implicit knowledge of reward associations. Conditions were composed of differentially-congruent emotion cues (happy face denoted as "H" vs. fear face denoted as "F") and reward outcomes (75% reinforced denoted by "+" vs. 25% reinforced denoted by "-"). Across age, test phase RT did not vary by what condition the choice was in the learning task. As demonstrated by the lack of significant differences between violin plots, familiar trials required comparable deliberation throughout the sample; the options were similarly distinct in every condition.

Supplemental Figure

Implicit and explicit knowledge of the reinforcement schedule of the emotion cues may differ from childhood to adulthood. To test for explicit knowledge, participants reported (using a slider from 0 to 45) how many reward outcomes they received for every face in the learning task. In this linear mixed-effects model, the reinforcement estimate for each face was predicted by age, In sum, we ruled out the possibility that goal-relevant, reward-based choice emerging with age could be explained by younger participants lacking implicit knowledge of the reinforcement structure of the learning task. However, our test of explicit knowledge, which was not confounded by age differences in the subjective valuation of money, offered two important insights. First, despite happy and fear faces having equivalent reinforcement rates, participants of all ages reported that happy faces produced reward outcomes more frequently than fear faces (main effect of emotion). Combined with EmoBias being the best-fitting model at the sample level, this result suggests that value integration occurred across valenced emotion and reward information and supports theories of composite value construction. Second, participants were more accurate at tracking relative reward outcomes with age (interaction between age and reward). While implicit knowledge of the reward associations formed during the learning task was apparent across the sample, explicit knowledge of these rewards improved from childhood to adulthood. Explicit knowledge of the reward structure of the task may underpin adults' preferences for leveraging previously goal-relevant reward information in the test phase.

Supplemental Figure S11. Subjective valuation of learning task-relevant amounts of money.

Younger participants rated small, task-relevant amounts of money as being more distinct while older participants rated small, task-relevant amounts of money as being more similar. Nonetheless, adults' behavior and self-report responses were most affected by the monetary reward manipulation in the learning task. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines.

Figure 1.

Figure 1. (A)Trial sequence in learning task. On each trial, participants saw a pair of card decks with faces on them. These faces displayed either the same or different emotions (happy vs. fear expressions) and matched participants' gender. Participants selected a deck, the card "flipped over," and they received feedback on their choice (50 vs. 0 cents) below a trial-unique image. This feedback was delivered according to a probabilistic reward structure (one deck in the pair was reinforced at 75% vs. the other was reinforced at 25%). (B) Orthogonal emotion-reward manipulation in learning task. Trials were embedded within four conditions, evenly dispersed throughout the task. One condition (H+F-) paired a 75% reinforced happy face deck with a 25% reinforced fear face deck, invoking congruent approach-avoid tendencies. Another condition (F+H-) paired a 75% reinforced fear face deck with a 25% reinforced happy face deck, invoking incongruent approach-avoid tendencies. The remaining conditions (H+H-, F+F-) paired 75% and 25% reinforced faces with the same emotional expressions; reward associations alone differentiated the card decks in these conditions. All face stimuli, including those shown here, are enumerated in Supplemental TableS1. (C) Measures in vertical order of administration.

Figure 2.

Figure 2. (A) Accuracy by trial number and age. Accuracy improved across time, especially with increasing age. (B) Accuracy by trial number and condition.Conditions were composed of differentially congruent emotion cues (happy face denoted as "H" vs. fear face denoted as "F") and reward outcomes (75% reinforced denoted by "+" vs. 25% reinforced denoted by "-"). Emotion-reward incongruence hindered accuracy more in earlier trials. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines. > annotations represent significantly different (Supplemental TableS8) condition-wise contrasts in estimated marginal means tests at targeted time points in learning.

Figure 3.

Figure3. Q-value differences by trial number, condition, and age group. Conditions were composed of differentially congruent emotion cues (happy face denoted as "H" vs. fear face denoted as "F") and reward outcomes (75% reinforced denoted by "+" vs. 25% reinforced denoted by "-"). Incidental emotion cues affected learning from integral reward outcomes in all ages, but children were impacted the longest by the congruency of the information sources. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around smoothed curves. Participants were binned into children (8 to 12 years), adolescents (13 to 17 years), and adults (18 to 22 years) to interpret changing patterns of Q-differences across age.

Figure 4.

Figure 4. Reward-based choice increased with age. (A) High-reward (75% reinforced) face decks were chosen more frequently and low-reward (25% reinforced) face decks were chosen less frequently into adolescence and adulthood. (B) Low-reward face decks were chosen more slowly into adulthood. (C) Emotion-matches explained greater deliberation time in children while reward-matches explained greater deliberation time in adults. Both emotion-and reward-matches explained greater deliberation time in adolescence. > annotations represent significant differences (Supplemental TableS9) between fullymatched pairs and other match types in estimated marginal means tests at targeted ages. Shading represents 95% confidence intervals around fitted lines.

Figure S6 Reward Associations Persisted to Bias Face Valence Ratings with Increasing Age….. Histogram of the participant sample (N = 121). Age was treated as a continuous variable in all mixed-effects models. Age was binned into children (8-12 years), adolescents (13-17 years), and adults (18-22 years) when interpreting temporal difference computational models.
